OnePlus phones hide one extra switch before a USB drive works at all — OxygenOS turns OTG off unless you enable it. Once it's on, a USB-C flash drive plugs straight in, and the stock route is My Files. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Xiaomi photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Xiaomi phones hide one extra switch before a USB drive works at all — on many MIUI and HyperOS phones OTG is off unless you enable it, and it switches itself off after about 10 minutes. Once it's on, a USB-C flash drive plugs straight in, and the stock route is Mi File Manager. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Huawei phones have their own built-in backup app, but it writes an encrypted archive — not plain photos you can open anywhere. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in, and the stock route is the Files app. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Sony Xperia photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Sony's Xperia phones run near-stock Android with almost no Sony apps on board — Google Photos is the gallery, Files by Google is the file manager — and Sony's own backup tool needs a PC. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up LG photos to a USB drive (no computer)

LG stopped making phones in 2021, so no LG guide or LG tool will ever be written for this — and LG's own backup tools move data phone-to-phone or phone-to-PC, never phone-to-USB as plain photos. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Oppo photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Oppo's ColorOS hides one switch between you and a USB drive — OTG Connection, which turns itself off after about 10 minutes unused — and Oppo's own backup tools move photos to another phone or to the cloud, never to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in once the switch is on. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in once the switch is on. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Vivo photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Vivo's Funtouch OS hides one switch between you and a USB drive — OTG, under Settings > More settings, which turns itself off if it is not used in 5 minutes — and Vivo's own backup tools move photos to another phone, to a PC, or to the cloud, never to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in once the switch is on. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 16, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up Honor photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Honor's MagicOS has no documented OTG switch to hunt for — the drive mounts on plug-in on most models, with a "USB storage added" notification — and Honor's own backup tools write an encrypted backup package, move photos to another phone or a PC, or back them up to the cloud, never to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 16, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up Nokia photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Nokia phones (made by HMD Global) run near-stock Android — Android One on most models — with no OTG switch to hunt for: the drive mounts on plug-in on most models, with a "USB storage added" notification. And Nokia has no backup app of its own: HMD's own guides point to Google backup, which restores to a new phone or to the cloud, never to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Asus phones (Zenfone and ROG Phone) run ZenUI — close to stock Android on recent models — with no OTG switch to hunt for: the drive mounts on plug-in on most models, with a "USB storage added" notification. And Asus's own backup tool moves your data to a new Asus phone, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Infinix photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Infinix phones (Note, Hot, Zero, and GT series) run XOS — Infinix's Android skin — with OTG support that varies by model: most recent models mount the drive on plug-in with a “USB storage added” notification, but some budget models have no OTG at all. And Infinix's own backup tools back your photos up to the cloud or to a PC, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight in. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 17, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up Tecno photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Tecno phones (Spark, Camon, Pova, Phantom, and Pop series) run HiOS — Tecno's Android skin — with OTG support that varies by model: most recent models mount the drive on plug-in with a "USB storage added" notification, but support is not universal, and some older models list OTG in the specs without it working out of the box. And Tecno's own backup tools back your photos up to the cloud, to an SD card, or to a PC, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into a modern Tecno. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 17, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up Nothing Phone photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Nothing phones (Phone (1), Phone (2), Phone (2a), and the CMF Phone 1) run Nothing OS — a near-stock Android skin — with OTG support on the main Phone models: spec tables list USB Type-C 2.0 with OTG for the Phone (1), Phone (2), Phone (2a), and Phone (2a) Plus, and Nothing's own support site documents OTG as a feature — but the CMF Phone 1 is the exception to check, because spec tables disagree on whether it supports OTG at all. And Nothing's own backup tools are Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — which back your photos up to the cloud, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any Nothing phone. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 17, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up POCO photos to a USB drive (no computer)

POCO phones (F, X, and M series) run HyperOS — Xiaomi's Android skin, since POCO is Xiaomi's sub-brand — with OTG support on the F and X series: spec tables list USB Type-C 2.0 with OTG for the F7, F7 Pro, X7, and X7 Pro, and USB Type-C 3.2 with OTG for the F9 Ultra — but the budget M series is the exception to check, because spec tables disagree on whether the M7 supports OTG at all. And POCO's own backup tools are Xiaomi Cloud and Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — which back your photos up to the cloud, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any POCO phone. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 17, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up Redmi photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Redmi phones (Note, K, and A series) run HyperOS — Xiaomi's Android skin, since Redmi is Xiaomi's sub-brand — with OTG support across the Note 14 line: spec tables list USB Type-C 2.0 with OTG for the Note 14 Pro 4G and the Note 14 Pro 5G — but the microSD slot varies by model: the Note 14 Pro 4G has a microSDXC slot (uses the shared SIM slot) while the Note 14 Pro 5G has none. And Redmi's own backup tools are Xiaomi Cloud and Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — which back your photos up to the cloud, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any Redmi phone. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

And iQOO's own backup tools are iQOO Cloud (vivo Cloud) and Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — plus PC tools, which back your photos up to the cloud or to a computer, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any current iQOO phone. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

And ZTE has no backup tool of its own: the routes on a ZTE phone are Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — plus PC tools, which back your photos up to the cloud or to a computer, not to a USB drive as plain files. Back up Fairphone photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Fairphone phones (4, 5, and 6) run Fairphone OS — near-stock Android — with USB-C and OTG on all three: spec tables list USB Type-C 3.0 with OTG and DisplayPort for the Fairphone 4 and the Fairphone 5, and USB Type-C 2.0 with OTG for the Fairphone 6. And Fairphone has no backup tool of its own: the routes on a Fairphone are Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — plus PC tools and rooted backup apps, which back your photos up to the cloud, to a computer, or to another device, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any current Fairphone. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Sharp Aquos photos to a USB drive (no computer)

And Sharp has no backup tool of its own: the routes on a Sharp Aquos are Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — plus, on Japan carrier models, docomo Data Copy to a microSD card, which back your photos up to the cloud, to a card, or to another phone, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any current Aquos. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 17, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up Lenovo photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Lenovo phones (K13, K13 Pro, and the Legion gaming phones) have USB-C and OTG across the range: spec tables list USB Type-C 2.0 with OTG for the K13, USB-C with OTG for the K13 Pro, and two USB-C ports with OTG for the Legion Phone Duel and the Legion 2 Pro — and even the budget K12 has OTG over micro-USB. And Lenovo has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: its own tool is Software Fix (formerly Rescue and Smart Assistant), a Windows PC app that backs your pictures up to a PC before a rescue, and the other routes are Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — which back your photos up to the cloud, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any USB-C Lenovo. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up HTC photos to a USB drive (no computer)

HTC phones (U23 Pro, Desire 22 Pro, and U23) have USB-C and OTG across the current range: spec tables list USB-C 3.0 with OTG for the U23 Pro, USB-C 2.0 with OTG for the Desire 22 Pro, and USB-C 3.0 with OTG for the U23 — and HTC's own support pages for the U12+ and the U12 life describe viewing files from a USB drive over OTG. And HTC has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: its own tool is HTC Sync Manager, a desktop app that backs your pictures up to a PC — and it is discontinued, support ended — and the other routes are Google's — Google Photos and Google backup — which back your photos up to the cloud, not to a USB drive as plain files. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any current HTC. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Blackview photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Blackview rugged phones (BV9300, BV9300 Pro, and BV6200 Pro) have USB-C and OTG across the range: spec tables list USB Type-C with OTG for the BV9300, OTG reverse charging for the BV9300 Pro, and OTG for the BV6200 Pro — and Blackview's own backup guide lists USB OTG as an external-storage option you can copy files to by hand. And Blackview has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: its own guide's six ways are a Google account backup, a local backup to a computer, third-party backup apps, a manual copy to external storage, ADB, and custom recovery — none of them an automated phone-to-USB copy. A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any current Blackview. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

A USB-C flash drive plugs straight into any current Ulefone. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 18, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up RugGear photos to a USB drive (no computer)

RugGear's Android phones (RG655, RG850, and RG880) have USB host support on at least the RG655 — the RG655 spec table lists USB OTG as Yes, the RG310 lists USB on-the-go, and the RG935 tablet lists OTG as supported — and RugGear has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: the RG655 manual gives no backup advice at all, and the stock photo backup is Google Photos. One honest caveat up front: the RG850 and RG880 datasheets I found do not list OTG either way, so check with a drive before relying on it, and the RG655 has a micro USB port, so it needs a micro-USB OTG adapter while the RG850 and RG880 are USB-C. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 18, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up Kyocera photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Kyocera's DuraForce Android phones (Pro 2, Ultra 5G, PRO 3, and DuraSport) have USB host support on at least two of them — the spec tables list USB OTG as Yes for the DuraForce Pro 2, and the DuraSport's spec pages list its port as "USB Type-C, USB On-The-Go" — and Kyocera has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: its backup advice is a computer copy or Google Drive and Google Photos in the cloud. One honest caveat up front: the Ultra 5G and PRO 3 datasheets list USB-C without mentioning OTG either way, so check Settings > Storage before relying on it on those models, and the older DuraForce phones are out of scope — the original DuraForce runs Android 4.4, the DuraForce XD runs Android 5.1, and the original DuraForce PRO's spec tables list USB OTG as No. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 18, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up Sonim photos to a USB drive (no computer)

One honest caveat up front: the XP10 datasheet I found does not list OTG either way, so check Settings > Storage before relying on it, and the XP5s is not an Android phone at all — it runs a proprietary OS, so SnapDisk cannot install on it. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Crosscall photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Crosscall's rugged Android phones (Core-X5, Action-X5, Core-Z5, and Stellar-X5) have USB host support on at least three of them — the spec tables list USB On-The-Go support as Yes for the Core-X5, Crosscall's own product sheet lists OTG as Yes for the Action-X5, and the Core-Z5 spec tables list USB On-The-Go support as Yes — and Crosscall has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: its backup advice is a computer copy or Google Drive and Google Photos in the cloud. One honest caveat up front: the Stellar-X5 spec pages I found list USB-C without mentioning OTG either way, so check Settings > Storage before relying on it on that model, and the older Trekker-M1 phones are out of scope — they run Android 5.1, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up Hammer photos to a USB drive (no computer)

Hammer's rugged Android phones (Blade V 5G, Iron 4, Blade 3, and Explorer Pro) have USB-C ports, and the only per-model OTG listings I found are FindPare's — USB OTG as Yes for the Energy 18x9 and the Blade 2 Pro — but FindPare itself says its information is not ascertained, and the current models' spec pages list USB-C without mentioning OTG either way, so check Settings > Storage before relying on it — and Hammer has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: its backup advice is a computer copy or Google Drive and Google Photos in the cloud. One honest caveat up front: the Hammer 6 LTE is a feature phone, not an Android phone at all, so SnapDisk cannot install on it, and the original Hammer Iron runs Android 4.2 and the AXE M LTE runs Android 6.0, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 18, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up FOSSiBOT photos to a USB drive (no computer)

FOSSiBOT's rugged F-series Android phones (F101, F101 Pro, F102, F107 Pro, F110 Pro 5G, F112 Pro, and F113) list USB OTG support in their spec tables, and FOSSiBOT has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: its backup advice is a computer copy or Google Drive and Google Photos in the cloud. Two honest caveats up front: the F101P's official page mentions OTG only as reverse charging — powering another device from the phone's battery, not hosting a drive — and the F115 Ultra's spec data conflicts (one source lists USB OTG as false while also listing USB-C as false), so check Settings > Storage on those two models before relying on it; and the original F101 went on sale in October 2020, and I did not verify its Android version in the sources above, so owners of that model should check Settings > About phone — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 18, 2026 · 4 min read

Back up UMIDIGI Bison photos to a USB drive (no computer)

UMIDIGI's rugged Bison line (Bison, Bison Pro, Bison Plus, Bison 2 Pro, Bison X10, Bison X10G, and Bison X10 Pro) lists USB OTG support in its spec tables, and UMIDIGI has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own: its backup advice is a computer copy or Google Drive and Google Photos in the cloud. Three honest caveats up front: the Bison X20's OTG host support is unverified — one source lists OTG Support as No, the others list USB Type-C only, and the reviews mention OTG reverse charging only — and the Bison GT, the Bison GT2 Pro, the Bison GT2 Pro 5G, the Bison X10S, and the Bison X10S NFC have no OTG listing in the sources I found, so check Settings > Storage on those models before relying on it; the original Bison's connector itself is disputed between sources — GSMArena and MySmartPrice list USB-C, while mobility.com.ng lists microUSB — so check your own port before buying an adapter; and I did not verify the Android version of the Bison X10, X10G, or X10 Pro in the sources above, so owners of those models should check Settings > About phone — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up photos from a broken-screen phone to USB

A broken screen is three different problems, and only two of them have a phone-to-USB route at all: a cracked screen with dead touch, where you can still see the display; a black screen with the phone still powering on, where you cannot see anything; and a completely dead phone, where no software route exists. This article covers all three, in that order, and it is the first article in this series that is about a situation instead of a phone brand — the brand series ran to 134 articles, and a broken screen can happen to any of them. Eighteen honest caveats up front: the three scenarios need three different routes, and only the first two have a phone-to-USB route at all; an OTG mouse unlocks nothing by itself — it only lets you enter your own PIN or pattern with a cursor, and it needs a screen you can still see; one port holds either the mouse or the drive, not both — a multi-port OTG hub, or Pro's auto-backup on connect, is what lets mouse and drive share the port; video-out needs DisplayPort Alt Mode — a USB 2.0 port physically cannot carry video, and most budget phones lack it; Samsung DeX is the best-documented video-out route — the S8 and later S series, the Note 8 and later, all Z Fold models and the A90 5G only — while the Z Flip 5 and 6 output video but run no DeX, per XDA, and the rest of the A series outputs nothing; Pixel video-out starts at the Pixel 8 — the Pixel 6 and 7 have USB 3.x ports but Google left display output switched off until the Pixel 8; OnePlus video-out is contested — the 12, 13 and Open per the usbctohdmi guide, against the 7 and newer flagships per the salivity list — so check your exact model before buying a hub; Motorola's desktop mode is Ready For, now Smart Connect, on Edge phones, and Huawei's is Easy Projection on Mate and P Pro phones; an on-screen prompt can block video-out — if you cannot see or tap it, the hub route stops there; Android 16's native desktop mode is rolling out — Pixel 8, 9 and 10 with Android 16 QPR3 or later, per Rayneo — so an older Pixel mirrors instead of opening a desktop; a completely dead phone has no software route — Android Authority says so plainly — and screen replacement or professional recovery is what is left; a microSD card comes out only if the phone takes one — most high-end phones no longer do, per Android Authority — and reading the card still needs a reader and a computer; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — one notification tap, which the mouse can do — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect, but only if it was set up before the screen broke; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README; check Google Photos first — the library may already be safe in the cloud — but Storage saver compresses photos, so check that setting before trusting a cloud copy; iPhones are not covered here — SnapDisk is Android only — and the iPhone OTG route varies by model, per the repair-shop guide; and the search results for this query are four results — an Android Authority generic broken-phone recovery guide, a repair-shop guide that does mention OTG-mouse unlock and copying photos to a USB drive, and two Dr.Fone guides — none of them is a whole-library one-tap phone-to-USB backup guide for a broken screen, and none of them mentions SnapDisk. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 20, 2026 · 10 min read

Back up photos to USB when switching to a new phone

A new phone is the moment most people find out whether their photos were really backed up. The transfer usually works — but "usually" is doing a lot of work in that sentence, and if the old phone has already been wiped or handed back for trade-in, there is no second chance. This article is about the step that comes before the transfer: a checked copy of the whole photo library on a USB drive you hold in your hand, made on the old phone, with no computer — and what to check before the old phone goes. Fourteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return a switching-specific guide — coticsy.com's "How to Back Up Your Photos Before Switching to a New Phone" — it covers the switch generally and USB copy as one manual step (exFAT, Files app, manual select/copy), and it is the best guide in the results; SnapDisk's own generic guides (transfer-photos-to-usb-automatically, usb-photo-backup) and FotoSafe's generic guide and a YouTube manual-OTG video also surface; NONE of them is a whole-library one-tap phone-to-USB backup guide framed for the phone-switch moment, and none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; SnapDisk is Android-only, so the iPhone half of a switch is the manual copy or a rival app, not SnapDisk; a transfer moves data, it is not a backup — it can be interrupted, skip items, or depend on photos that are not actually on the old phone because they live only in the cloud; the cloud-only originals trap is real in both directions — iCloud Photos with Optimize iPhone Storage keeps space-saving versions on the phone and the originals in iCloud, and Google Photos with Storage saver compresses photos larger than 16 MP down to 16 MP and videos over 1080p down to 1080p, per Google's own support page — so download the originals first; an exact file copy preserves the capture dates and locations already embedded in the originals, per the coticsy guide; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT if both phones support it, and test the drive with both phones before the switch; SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the wipe step is always yours, and always after the check; and the search results for this query are five results — SnapDisk's own generic guide, the coticsy switching guide, SnapDisk's generic USB guide, FotoSafe's generic guide, and a YouTube manual-OTG video — none of them is a whole-library one-tap phone-to-USB backup guide framed for the phone-switch moment. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 20, 2026 · 9 min read

Back up photos from an SD card to USB (no computer)

Your photos may live on a card, not in the phone — on the microSD card in the phone's slot, or on the full-size SD card in your camera. Either way the copy to a USB drive can be made with the phone alone, no computer — but the two cards are not the same job, and the guides that already exist only cover one of them. Fourteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real guides — five of them — brightideaphotography.com, desnoulez.fr, petapixel.com, Ask Tim Grey, and goldenlenstraveler.com; all five are about a camera's memory card copied to an SSD with the phone as a bridge, by hand in the Files app; none of them is about the phone's own microSD card; none of them is a whole-library one-tap backup; and none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; "SD card" means two different things here — the microSD card in your phone and the full-size SD card in your camera — and this article covers the phone's card first, then the camera's card; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; the From folder is a picker, not a fixed path — tap it, browse to the card, and choose the card's DCIM folder, which the picker reaches because it is the system folder picker, verified in the repo as OpenDocumentTree; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT, which the bridge guides recommend too; one USB port holds either the card reader or the drive, not both — copying straight from card to drive needs a powered hub, or two steps through the phone's storage; SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the card stays a second copy until you have opened photos on the drive and checked them; an iPhone has no microSD slot, so the phone's-card route is Android only, and the camera-card route on iPhone is the Files app, not SnapDisk; and a card formatted by a camera may use a folder layout other than DCIM — check the card's folders before picking, and pick the folder that actually holds the photos. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 20, 2026 · 9 min read

Back up photos from a tablet to USB (no computer)

A tablet is not a big phone when it comes to backup — its library is often years of family photos, many Android tablets have a microSD slot that high-end phones no longer do, older tablets still use micro-USB while newer ones use USB-C, and whether the tablet supports USB OTG varies by model. The copy itself is the same job — tablet to drive, no computer — but the port, the slot, and the size change the steps. Fourteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real tablet guides — five of them — ceedo.com, petapixel.com, thecomputerbasics.com, ssa-digital.com, and pixoneye.com; all five are manual select-copy-paste guides, and none of them is a whole-library one-tap backup; none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; tablets differ from phones — often larger libraries, a microSD slot on many Android tablets but never on an iPad, USB-C on newer tablets and micro-USB on older ones, and OTG support that varies by model; the phone-bridge pattern from the SD-card article does not apply the same way — the tablet holds the photos itself, so the copy is direct tablet-to-drive, and the bridge only reappears for a camera card; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; the screenshot below is from SnapDisk on a phone, not a tablet — I have not run the app on a tablet, so the tablet layout is unverified even though the settings screen is the same screen; the From folder is a picker, not a fixed path — tap it, browse to the tablet's storage or its card, and choose the DCIM folder, which the picker reaches because it is the system folder picker, verified in the repo as OpenDocumentTree; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT; a tablet supplies limited power over its port — a USB stick or an SSD is fine, but a spinning hard drive may appear and then disconnect, so use a powered hub for one; an iPad has no microSD slot and SnapDisk is Android only, so the iPad route is the Files app, not SnapDisk; a tablet's photos may not all sit in DCIM — check the tablet's folders before picking, and pick the folder that actually holds the photos; and SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the tablet stays a second copy until you have opened photos on the drive and checked them. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

November 20, 2026 · 9 min read

Back up photos from a Chromebook to USB (no computer)

A Chromebook is not an Android phone with a keyboard — it runs Android apps inside ChromeOS, only some Chromebooks have Google Play at all, its photos live in three places at once, and whether an Android app can even see a USB drive depends on a setting most owners have never opened. Thirteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real Chromebook guides — five of them — asurion.com, chromeready.com twice, itigic.com, and howtogeek.com; all five are manual select-copy-paste guides, and none of them is a whole-library one-tap backup; none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; a Chromebook runs Android apps only if it has Google Play, and Google says the Play Store is available on only some Chromebooks — check Settings, Apps, Manage Google Play preferences, and if it is missing, the Chromebook does not run Android apps; SnapDisk has never been run on a Chromebook — whether its folder picker reaches a USB drive on ChromeOS is unverified, and ChromeOS keeps Android apps away from external storage until you turn the drive on under Settings, System preferences, Storage management, External storage preference; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; the screenshot below is from SnapDisk on a phone, not a Chromebook — I have not run the app on a Chromebook, so the Chromebook layout is unverified even though the settings screen is the same screen; the From folder is a picker, not a fixed path — tap it, browse to the Chromebook's storage, and choose the folder that holds the photos, which the picker reaches because it is the system folder picker, verified in the repo as OpenDocumentTree — but that reach on ChromeOS is unverified, so check the drive appears in the picker before trusting the copy; a Chromebook's photos may not all sit in one place — some in Downloads, some in Google Drive, some only in Google Photos in the cloud — and a cloud-only photo is not on the Chromebook to copy, so download the originals first; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT, which ChromeOS reads and writes; a school or work Chromebook may block the Play Store or USB storage entirely — an administrator can switch both off — so check with the administrator before buying anything; and SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the Chromebook stays a second copy until you have opened photos on the drive and checked them. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up photos to USB when phone storage is full

A full phone is not a phone with a lot of photos — it is a phone that has stopped working properly: the camera will not save, apps will not install or update, and the file-manager move you would reach for can fail halfway and leave photos in neither place. Thirteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real storage-full guides — seven of them — zoriumdaily.com, mobiletrans.wondershare.com, innoparkit.com, thetechedvocate.org, gigagone.com, fonelab.com, and eyefi.com; our own free-up-space article already covers moving photos to a USB drive to free space — this one is different, it is about the moment the phone is already full, when the camera will not save and apps will not install; a full phone cannot take a photo, record a video, or update an app — the zoriumdaily and thetechedvocate guides say so plainly; a phone with less than about one or two gigabytes free will often struggle to update apps or save new photos, per zoriumdaily; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; the screenshot below is from SnapDisk on a phone — it is the same settings screen you will see, because this article is about a phone; the From folder is a picker, not a fixed path — tap it, browse to the phone's storage, and choose the folder that holds the photos, which the picker reaches because it is the system folder picker, verified in the repo as OpenDocumentTree; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT; if the phone is too full to install anything at all, free a sliver first — clear an app cache or delete one big video you already have elsewhere — then install SnapDisk; and SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the phone stays a second copy until you have opened photos on the drive and checked them. Back up photos to USB when your phone battery is low

A low battery is a clock, not a wall — the photos are still on the phone, the drive is still in your hand, and the copy is still a local one that needs no Wi-Fi and no upload. Fourteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real guides — our own offline-backup and external-drive articles, mobiletrans.wondershare.com, a Samsung community thread, and a Photostock OmniX review — plus four battery-advice guides, fonelab.com, invotech.dut.ac.za, sekin.in, and eztoolset.com; all of them are manual select-copy-paste, one-line battery advice, or a $34.99 hardware product, and none of them is a whole-library one-tap backup framed for the low-battery moment, and none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; SnapDisk's copy runs as a foreground service — the mechanism Android gives a copy that must keep running with the screen off — but the repo holds no wake lock and no battery-saver handling, and no low-battery run has ever been tested, so screen-off continuation under battery saver is unverified, and this article says so instead of promising it; the drive occupies the phone's only USB-C port — the phone cannot charge from a power bank at the same time unless the connection goes through a USB-C hub with pass-through charging, which is standard USB-C behaviour but unverified with SnapDisk; if the phone dies mid-copy, nothing already copied is lost — finished files stay on the drive, the file in flight keeps its partial copy, and resume picks up where it stopped, verified in the repo; Android 15 and later give a dataSync foreground service 6 hours in 24 hours — if the copy runs past that, Android pauses it, and opening the app resets the timer; an OTG adapter drains the battery — fonelab says so plainly, charge first if you can — and the drive draws its power from the phone; a spinning hard drive may need its own power; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; the screenshot below is from SnapDisk on a phone — it is the same settings screen you will see, because this article is about a phone; the From folder is a picker, not a fixed path — tap it, browse to the phone's storage, and choose the folder that holds the photos, which the picker reaches because it is the system folder picker, verified in the repo as OpenDocumentTree; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT; a cloud backup is the slow way out of a low battery — it needs Wi-Fi, upload time, and free space in the account, and the phone burns battery uploading for hours; and SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the phone stays a second copy until you have opened photos on the drive and checked them. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up photos to USB before resetting your phone

A factory reset is the one button on the phone that cannot be undone — it erases every photo, app, message, and setting on the internal storage, and the photos are gone the moment it runs. Fourteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real guides — four phone-to-USB guides, fotosafe.weidisoft.net, a YouTube walkthrough, sekin.in, and umatechnology.org, plus four factory-reset guides, gadgets.beebom.com, androidauthority.com, memeburn.com, and everydaytech.org — and none of the eight is a whole-library one-tap phone-to-USB backup framed for the before-the-reset moment, and none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; the backup must COMPLETE before the reset — a reset wipes the phone including any on-device copy, and there is no undo, so the copy-then-check-then-reset order is the whole article; cloud-only originals are a trap — Google Photos backup can be off, device folders like WhatsApp and screenshots may never have been switched on, and storage saver uploads are compressed copies, not originals, so check photos.google.com before trusting the cloud, and a reset can strand anything that never uploaded; after the reset, Android asks for the Google account that was on the phone before — Factory Reset Protection — so know the account password before resetting, or the phone can lock you out of your own device; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT; a spinning hard drive may need its own power; SnapDisk's copy runs as a foreground service — the mechanism Android gives a copy that must keep running with the screen off — but the repo holds no wake lock and no battery-saver handling, and no reset-prep run has ever been tested, so screen-off continuation under battery saver is unverified, and this article says so instead of promising it; Android 15 and later give a dataSync foreground service 6 hours in 24 hours — if the copy runs past that, Android pauses it, and opening the app resets the timer; a factory reset erases the phone's internal storage, not a USB drive — but unplug the drive before tapping reset anyway, because the reset is the moment a mistake costs the most; and SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the phone stays a second copy until you have opened photos on the drive and checked them. Back up photos to USB before you sell your phone

Selling or trading in a phone ends with a factory reset — and a reset erases every photo on the phone, with no undo once the phone is wiped or handed over. Fifteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real guides — techsith's get-pictures-off guide, a MacRumors forum thread, a Facebook reel, an Instagram reel, plus our own Unitech article — and none of the five is a whole-library one-tap phone-to-USB backup framed for the sell-or-trade-in moment, and none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; the backup must COMPLETE and be verified on the drive BEFORE the wipe or the hand-over — no undo, so the copy-then-check-then-wipe order is the whole article; trade-in and carrier programs require a factory reset before hand-over, so back up first, verify second, wipe last; cloud-only originals are a trap — Google Photos backup can be off, device folders like WhatsApp and screenshots may never have been switched on, and storage saver uploads are compressed copies, not originals, so check photos.google.com before trusting the cloud, and a reset can strand anything that never uploaded; remove your accounts before the reset — take the Google account off in Settings, and the Samsung account too if the phone has one — or Factory Reset Protection asks the buyer for your password at setup; take the SIM, the microSD card, and the eSIM out or off before handing the phone over — a factory reset does not wipe a microSD card, and the card can still hold your photos; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; the screenshot below is from SnapDisk on a phone — it is the same settings screen you will see, because this article is about a phone; the From folder is a picker, not a fixed path — tap it, browse to the phone's storage, and choose the folder that holds the photos, which the picker reaches because it is the system folder picker, verified in the repo as OpenDocumentTree; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT; a spinning hard drive may need its own power; SnapDisk's copy runs as a foreground service — the mechanism Android gives a copy that must keep running with the screen off — but the repo holds no wake lock and no battery-saver handling, and no selling-prep run has ever been tested, so screen-off continuation under battery saver is unverified, and this article says so instead of promising it; Android 15 and later give a dataSync foreground service 6 hours in 24 hours — if the copy runs past that, Android pauses it, and opening the app resets the timer; and SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — deleting photos from the phone afterwards is your step, only after you have opened photos on the drive and checked them. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Back up photos to USB while traveling (no Wi-Fi)

On the road there is no cloud to fall back on and no second copy — the phone is the only copy until the drive copy finishes and is checked, so the backup has to COMPLETE and be verified before the phone is wiped, lost, or the trip ends. Fifteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real travel-backup guides — four of them — PetaPixel's "No Laptop? No Problem", desnoulez.fr, shawnmoreton.com, and PurrfectBackup — plus the same-shape manual phone-to-USB guides, diyphotography.net, thegadgetians.com, and mefmobile.org, and our own OnePlus article — and none of them is a whole-library one-tap backup of the phone's own photos framed for the traveling, no-Wi-Fi, no-computer moment, and none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; the three bridge guides back up a CAMERA card, using the phone as a bridge between the card and an SSD — this article backs up the phone's own library, which is a different job, and the bridge route gets Method 4; PurrfectBackup is dedicated pocket hardware that copies memory cards to an SSD with no phone, no Wi-Fi, and no apps — but it is pre-launch, not yet shipping, and it copies cards, not the phone's library; cloud-only originals are a trap — Google Photos backup can be off, device folders like WhatsApp and screenshots may never have been switched on, and storage saver uploads are compressed copies, not originals, so check photos.google.com before trusting the cloud — and on the road with no Wi-Fi the upload cannot run at all, so the drive copy comes first and the cloud check waits for Wi-Fi; one USB-C port holds the drive OR the charger, not both — charge first, or use a powered hub with pass-through charging, which is standard USB-C behaviour but unverified with SnapDisk; a portable SSD may need a powered hub — the phone alone may not supply enough power for both the hub and the drive; SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later — minSdk 24 in the repo — and it is not on Google Play yet, so install the v0.1.2 APK from GitHub; the free version backs up manually up to 5 GB per run — run it again past the cap and it picks up where it stopped, because files already copied are never copied twice — and Pro is a one-time $9.99 purchase that auto-starts the backup on connect and removes the size limit; the screenshot below is from SnapDisk on a phone — it is the same settings screen you will see, because this article is about a phone; the From folder is a picker, not a fixed path — tap it, browse to the phone's storage, and choose the folder that holds the photos, which the picker reaches because it is the system folder picker, verified in the repo as OpenDocumentTree; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT; a spinning hard drive may need its own power; SnapDisk's copy runs as a foreground service — the mechanism Android gives a copy that must keep running with the screen off — but the repo holds no wake lock and no battery-saver handling, and no travel run has ever been tested, so screen-off continuation under battery saver is unverified, and this article says so instead of promising it; Android 15 and later give a dataSync foreground service 6 hours in 24 hours — if the copy runs past that, Android pauses it, and opening the app resets the timer; and SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the phone stays a second copy until you have opened photos on the drive and checked them, and the drive stays in a different bag from the phone. Back up photos to USB before your Android update

Your phone is offering an Android update, and the update itself almost never wipes your photos — but a failed or stuck update, a bootloop, or a factory reset during troubleshooting does, and after a wipe there is no undo. Fifteen honest caveats up front: the exact query DOES return real guides — five of them — FotoSafe's guided app backup, mefmobile.org, a YouTube OTG guide, umatechnology.org, and Dr.Fone's four-method guide — and none of the five is a whole-library one-tap phone-to-USB backup framed for the before-an-Android-update moment, and none of them mentions SnapDisk — that angle is the gap this article fills; an Android update rarely wipes user data — that is the plain fact, and the backup here is insurance against a failed update, a bootloop, or a reset during troubleshooting, not against the update itself; the backup must COMPLETE and be verified on the drive BEFORE the update starts — a copy still running when the phone restarts for the update is not a backup, and there is no undo after a wipe; cloud-only originals are a trap — Google Photos backup can be off, device folders like WhatsApp and screenshots may never have been switched on, and storage saver uploads are compressed copies, not originals, so check photos.google.com before trusting the cloud — and with no Wi-Fi the upload cannot run at all, so the drive copy comes first and the cloud check waits for Wi-Fi; drives formatted FAT32 cannot hold a single file over 4 GB — SnapDisk skips those files and lists them instead of failing the whole backup, per the repo README — so use exFAT; a spinning hard drive may need its own power; SnapDisk's copy runs as a foreground service — the mechanism Android gives a copy that must keep running with the screen off — but the repo holds no wake lock and no battery-saver handling, and no before-a-flight run has ever been tested, so screen-off continuation under battery saver is unverified, and this article says so instead of promising it; Android 15 and later give a dataSync foreground service 6 hours in 24 hours — if the copy runs past that, Android pauses it, and opening the app resets the timer; and SnapDisk copies, it never deletes — the phone stays a second copy until you have opened photos on the drive and checked them, and the drive stays in a different bag from the phone once the copy is done. Here is the manual way, and the one-tap version.

Save Reddit photos to USB from Android — SnapDisk

Reddit's Save button only bookmarks the post — tap the photo, use the three-dot menu, and the file lands in Pictures/Reddit. Then back it up to USB.

Your Drive photos are files in the cloud, not files on your phone. Download them from the Drive app — they land in Downloads — then back them up to USB.
